CHAPTER XII FRACTIONAL EQUATIONS 

251. When multiplying or adding fractions you usually get a fraction as a result. Only in some cases will the product or sum happen to be a whole number. In solving an equation containing fractions, however, the first step is to change the equation into a new equation which does not contain fractions. This is called clearing of fractions. 

To change a fractional equation into a new equation which does not contain fractions, multiply every term of the equation by the L. C. M. of the denominators. 3 

EXAMPLE. Solve x+1 x² - 2x 

x + 11 6 x 12 

First, factor all the denominators. Then you see that a suitable multiplier would be 6 x(x − 2) because this expression is divisible by every denominator. The multiplier is written in front of each term in the equation, and parentheses are placed around each binomial. 6 x(x-2) 3 x(x

PROBLEMS 

The usefulness of these exercises lies chiefly in the training which they give in finding the equations. The problems in this list lead to quadratic equations. All other types of problems were studied in 

Chapters V and VI. 

Rate Problems (Review Page 95.) 

In the following rate problems, the equation can be formed by comparing either the times or the rates. In the first problem, for example, find the time required as the man did travel and find the time as he might have traveled. 

1. A man traveled 30 mi. If he had gone 5 mi. an hour faster, he would have performed the trip in 1 hr. less. Find his rate in miles an hour. 

2. Some people took a trip of 70 mi. They traveled 10 mi. within the city and 60 mi. outside the city. The rate outside the city was 10 mi. an hour faster than the rate within the city. If the trip took 24 hr., find the rates. 10 r, then is the number of hours If the rate in the city is r, in the city. What is the rate outside the city? What is the number of hours outside the city? The equation should state that the sum of these hours is 5. 

If you could not do ex. 1 when you first tried it, try it again now. 

3. A man rode 2 mi. in a car, and then returned on a car going 2 mi. an hour faster. If he spent 22 min. riding, how fast did each car go? 

4. A man travels 120 mi. by one train and returns on a train going 8 mi. an hour faster. The total time for his trip is 5 hr. Find the rate of each train. 

5. The distance from Chicago to St. Louis is 285 mi.

When the rate of a train going from one city to the other is decreased 5 mi. an hour, the running time is increased 1 hr. 

Find the usual rate of the train.

It can go 8 mi. up a river and return in 1 hr. 40 min. What is the rate of the current? 

8. Some boys are canoeing on a river in part of which the current is 5 mi. an hour and in another part 3 mi. an hour. 

If, when going downstream, they go 4 mi. where the current is rapid and 8 mi. where the current is slower in a total of 

1 hr. 50 min., what is their rate in still water?

CHAPTER XIII LITERAL EQUATIONS 

254. Problems Containing Literal Numbers. In earlier work certain rules or formulas were used to find areas or circumferences of circles, etc. We can use formulas also to help us solve more rapidly certain types of problems, as will be shown below. 

Solve these four problems: 

1. The difference of two numbers is 19. Find the numbers if their sum is 53. 

2. Find two numbers whose difference is 37 and sum 69. 

3. Find two numbers one of which exceeds the other by 

13, their sum being 127. 

4. If one number is 40 larger than another and their sum is 90, what are the numbers?

These four problems are alike because in each one we are looking for two numbers and we know their difference and their sum.

Hence let us call their sum s and their difference d. In the first problem d 19 and s = 53. What are the values of d and s in ex. 2, 3, and 4?

If we examine the answers to each problem, we shall find that the larger of the two unknown numbers is always one half the sum of s and d, and the smaller is always one half the difference of s and d. In other words: 

Larger number = 

Smaller number = (s + d) (sd) 253

LITERAL EQUATIONS 

We can prove that these rules are correct by solving one of the problems, using the literal numbers s and d. 255. 

Let the larger number = n 

Then the smaller number = n Since the sum of the numbers is s, the equation is 

= s n + n d 

Transposed to the right: 2 n = s + d n = 1 (s + d)

257. To solve the equation 3 x ac = we first write the equation as 3 x + ax = c2ax for x, c² + ac. If now the left member were 3 x + 5 x we should add these terms, getting 8x; but how shall we add 3 x + ax?

The terms 3 x and ax are alike in one respect: they both involve the number x. If the question were asked, "How many times is x multiplied in these two terms?" the answer would be, "3 times and a times, or (3 + a) times." Hence 3x + ax = (3+ a)x We have previously called such work factoring and written:

3x + ax = x(3+ a) In the term 3 x the number 3 is called the coefficient of x. 

In the term ax the number a is the coefficient of x. In a 

6 bx the coefficient of x is 

- 6 b. The coefficient term like tells by what number x is multiplied.

To add several terms containing x, add the coefficients of x in the various terms, and then indicate the product of x by the sum of the coefficients.

CHAPTER XIV SQUARE ROOTS 

263. Problem. What is the length of a square whose area is 500 sq. ft.?

The number of square feet in the area of a square is found by squaring the number of feet in the length. We find the length of the square by finding the square root of the number of square feet in the area. Hence in the above problem the length of the square is the square root of 500, usually written V500.

The sign is called a radical sign and means the square root" of the number under it. The number under the radical sign is called the radicand. 

5 = Every number has two square roots, even though we cannot always use both in a problem. Thus + 5 · + 5 = 25 and 5. 

25. The positive square root is called the principal root. The expression √25 means the positive square root; √25 means the negative square root. 

25 369 

Certain numbers, like 4, 9, 35, are called squares because their square roots can be found exactly. The square roots of other numbers, like 3, 5, 14, cannot be found exactly because no matter how far we carry out the work of finding the root, there is always a remainder. Such square roots are called irrational numbers. 

264. Square Roots of Numbers. To find √1860, for example, we first notice that the square root is a number between 40 and 50 because 402 = 1600 and 50² = 2500. 

Let us call the square root 40 +x. Then (40 + x)² 1860, 1860, or x² + 80 x = 260 or 

1600+80 x + x² 262

SQUARE ROOTS OF NUMBERS 

263 Although we cannot solve this equation by factoring, nevertheless the equation will suggest what to try next. 

If we omit the term x², then the rest of the equation, 80 x= 260, shows that x is nearly 3. Hence if we had guessed 43 at the start instead of 40, we should be nearer the correct value. If 43 is not correct, then we may write (43 + y)² = 1860 and use this new equation for the next trial, etc.

In the above work we notice two important steps: 

1. The square root lies between 40 and 50. 

2. After subtracting 402 from 1860, getting 260, we next divide 260 by twice 40, or 80.

By using the figures below we can see why these are the two important steps in the process. Finding the square root of 1860 is the same as finding the side of a square whose area is 1860. In the left-hand figure a 40-foot square is drawn inside the larger square, and an angle-shaped piece 40 40 40 40 remains. 

Its area is 1860 

40 

402, or 260. In the right-hand figure the angle-shaped piece is made into a rectangle. We can guess its approximate width by dividing its area, 260, by its approximate length, 80. Its actual length, however, is not 80, but 80 + x, so that if 3 is our guess for the value of x, then we must divide, not by 80, but by 80 + 3. Upon these facts is based the usual method of arithmetic for finding square roots.

SQUARE ROOTS 273. Addition of Like Radicals. Multiply √3 by 2. Also multiply √3 by 10. Next, multiply √3 by 12. Does 2√3 + 10√3 equal 12√3? The statement 2 x + 10 x 12 x is true for all numbers, including the number x = √3. We notice also that, just as we cannot add 5 x + 7y, so we cannot add 5√3 + 7√2 unless we find the value of each in decimals. 

Radicals like 2√3 and 5√3 are called like radicals because the radicands are the same. For a similar reason 

2√3 and 52 are called unlike radicals.

EXAMPLE. 

8√3+6√5 - √3 = 7√3 + 6√5 274.

SQUARE ROOTS THE PYTHAGOREAN EQUATION 

276. A right triangle is one in which one of the angles is a right angle. The side opposite the right angle is called the hypotenuse, and the other two sides are called arms.

The symbol for the word triangle is ▲; thus ▲ABC means the triangle whose corners are the points A, B, and C. In the figures below there are nine right triangles. Name them. State which lines are the hypotenuses.

L M 

Pythagoras proved that in any right triangle (one arm)² + (the other arm)² (hypotenuse)2

Write this statement more concisely by calling the length of one arm a, of the other arm b, and of the hypotenuse h.

This equation contains three numbers, a, b, and h. If we are told two of these numbers, we can find the third one by substituting their values in the equation and then solving the equation for the unknown number.

EXAMPLE. Find the width of a rectangle if it is 14 ft. long and the diagonal is 18 ft. 

From the figure we see that w² + 142 182 or w² + 196 = 324 Then w2 = 128 and w = √128 18 20 14 The value of V128 is not given in the table on page 269 but we can easily find it because √128 8√2.

279. Rationalizing a Denominator.

To find the value of √5 in decimals we must divide 2 by √5; that is, divide 

2 by 2.236. (See page 267.) This work can be made easier if we remove the radical from the denominator of the fraction by multiplying its numerator and denominator 2 √5 by √5. Thus, 2 √5 2 X √5 2√5 or 5 √5 X √5 5 Then instead of dividing by 2.236 we divide by 5.

2 Notice that while the first fraction, has a radical in √5' its denominator, the new fraction, 25, has a radical in its numerator. The process of changing a fraction (without altering its value) so that radicals will not be found in the denominator is called rationalizing the denominator. 280.

281. Historical Note. 

early part of the sixteenth century by Rudolff, a German mathematician, and is taken from the first letter of the Latin word radix meaning root. (Compare this with the origin of and as mentioned on page 41.) Similarly, ✓✓✓ denoted the cube root, and ✓✓ the fourth root. (Why should the triple sign √√√ be used for a cube root. and the double sign √√ for a fourth root?)

The relation a² + b² = h² (page 273) is one of the oldest known facts of arithmetic. Centuries before Christ the 

Egyptians laid out right angles by using a rope with knots so spaced that the lengths between the knots were in the ratio 3 to 4 to 5. To prove this relation a knowledge of geometry is necessary. Pythagoras, who died about 

500 B.C., is supposed to have made the first proof. then many hundreds of proofs have been given, one of which is credited to President Garfield.

(Of what Latin words 

Descartes did most to introduce our modern symbols. He wrote x3* + px + q° 0 for x3 + px + q 0. The* after r³ indicated that the next lower exponent was missing in the expression. Still earlier forms were: 1Z p. 5 Rm. 4 and 1 p. 50 m. 4 for x²+5x4. Wallis, an Englishman, showed in 1659 how fractions (see page 324) and even negative numbers may be used as exponents.

CHAPTER XV QUADRATIC EQUATIONS 282. Review of Trinomial Squares. A method of solving quadratic equations, which is often used when factoring is impossible, depends on using trinomials that are squares. The trinomial 9 x2 +30 xy + 25 y2 equals (3 x + 5 y) (3 x + 5 y). 

Since the two factors are the same we can write 

9x230 xy + 25 y²: =(3x+5y)2

When the two factors of a trinomial are the same, the trinomial is a square, and either factor is the square root of the trinomial. 

Such squares can be recognized at sight because three things must be true: (1) The first term must be a square. (2) The third or last term must also be a square. (3) The middle term must be twice the product of the square roots of the other terms.

Thus, 9 x²+30 xy + 25 y² is a square because: (1) The first term, 9 x2, is the square of 3 x. (2) The last term, 25 y², is the square of 5 y. (3) The middle term, 30 xy, is twice the product of these square roots. 30 xy equals 23 x 5 y. 

5y, Note that since the square root of 9 x² is either + 3x or - 3 x, and the square root of 25 y² is either + 5 y or the middle term might be either + 30 xy or 

the trinomial would still be a square.

9x230 xy + 25 y² = (3 x - 5 y)2 

30 xy, and 278

QUADRATIC EQUATIONS 289. A Formula for Solving Quadratic Equations. The value of x found from any equation, as 3 x2 +7x-5=0, depends on the numbers 3, 7, - 5 which appear in the equation. If any of these numbers are changed, the value of x is changed. 

If the coefficient of x² is a instead of 3, and the coefficient of x is b instead of 7, and the last term is c instead of — 5, the equation reads ax² + bx + c = 0 

Thus, for 7 x² + 9 x 4

PROBLEMS FOR REVIEW 

Some of these problems lead to quadratic equations, some to linear equations, and some to sets of equations. 

1. A baseball team has won 10 games and lost 7 games and still has 8 games to play. How many of the 8 games must the team win to finish with an average of .600? 

2. A coach wishes to add the same amount to the length and the width of an athletic field so that the diagonal of the field will be increased by 100 ft. If the field is now 

400 ft. by 300 ft., how many feet, to two decimal places, should be added to each dimension? 

3. A golf ball of radius r is packed for mailing in a cylindrical can.

If the ball fits exactly between the top and the bottom of the cylinder and touches its sides, show that the volume of the ball is of the volume of the cylinder. 

4. A crew that rows 8 mi. an hour downstream rows back the same distance in 333% more time. Find the rate of the current in the river. 

5. Some boys who are boating on a river know that they can go downstream 8 mi. an hour and go upstream 5 mi. an hour. How far may they go if they have 6 hr. for the trip? 

6. Some boys propose to find the rate of the current in a river as follows: They know that their motor boat can go 9 mi. an hour in still water. After going downstream for some time the boys find that it takes them twice as many minutes to return to the starting point. Find the rate of the current. 

How much money 

7. A man has $3000 invested at 4%. must he invest at 7% to make the total income equal to 6% on the total investment? 

8. A wheel d inches in diameter is making n revolutions a minute. Write a formula stating how many feet, f, a point on the rim of the wheel moves in a second.

PROBLEMS FOR REVIEW 

293 

9. A boy's grade in English was 90, in Science 85, and in Latin 85. What grade must he make in Algebra in order to average 86 in his four subjects? 

10. One pipe can fill a tank in 2 hr. and another pipe can fill the tank in 1 hr. 20 min. How long will it take to fill the tank if both pipes are running? 

11. A party of boys bought a canoe for $70, dividing the expense equally. If the number of boys had been 2 less, the expense per person would have been increased by $1.75. 

How many boys were there in the party? 

12. Divide $108 between A and B so that B will get 50% more than A. 

13. How much must be added to the radius of a circle whose diameter is 6 in. to make the area 4 times as large? 

14. The height of a flagstaff was found as follows: It was noticed that the rope fastened at the top was 5 ft. longer than the staff, and that when the rope was stretched tight, it reached the ground at a point 25 ft. from the base of the staff. How high was the flagstaff? 

15. A farmer is cutting grain around a square field 40 rd. long. Find to two decimals how wide a strip he must cut so as to cut of the grain. 

16. What sum of money invested at 6% simple interest for 4 yr. will amount to $620 ?

CHAPTER XVI NUMERICAL TRIGONOMETRY 

295. Problem. 

A surveyor wishes to measure the distance between the points R and S on opposite sides of a pond. 

First Solution. A Scale Drawing 

The surveyor selects a point T and measures the distances TR and TS. With a transit (see page 295) he measures angle T.

Suppose that TR 250 ft., TS 375 ft., and T = 73°. The surveyor then draws from his measurements a map or a plan, just as an architect draws a plan of a house. On his plan the surveyor lets 1 in. represent 100 ft. (The figure above is drawn to the scale 1 in. 400 ft.) How long must the line TS be on the surveyor's map if it is to represent 375 ft.? At the point T on his map he draws, with a protractor, an angle of 73°, and then draws a line to represent 

TR. How long does he make this line? With a ruler he can now measure the line RS on his map. If he finds that it is a trifle less than 37 in., what is the distance RS?

Second Solution. A Right Triangle 

The transit is first placed at S, pointed toward R, and the telescope is then turned 90°. The surveyor's assistant drives a stake T somewhere in "the line of sight." After measuring TR and TS, what equation can be used to find RS, since 

RST is a right triangle? If RT 600 ft. and TS 460 ft., find RS. 294

NUMERICAL TRIGONOMETRY 

295 Neither of the two methods described on the preceding page is very accurate. In the first method a very small error in the drawing of the plan would make a large actual error. An error of one sixteenth of an inch, for example, means an actual error of more than six feet because one inch represents one hundred feet. 

Each pupil should make a scale drawing, as explained in the first solution, in order to see how closely he can come to the correct length of RS, which is 385 ft. 

The second method can also be improved. As will be seen later, we need not measure two lines, TR and TS, but only one line and one angle. This is a decided advantage, because angles can be measured more easily than lines. 

296. The transit is an instrument for measuring the angle between two different directions or lines. Its essential parts are a telescope, through which the surveyor sees the object, and a horizontal and a vertical protractor. The telescope is first pointed toward one of the objects and the reading on the protractor is noted; the telescope is then turned so as to point to the other object. The scale on the protractor then shows how many degrees the telescope was turned. In some problems (ex. 6, page 301) corrections must be made because the telescope is not on the level with the ground.

DEFINITION OF TANGENT 

297 298. Definition of Tangent.

In the above exercises the BC ratio will be different for each angle, but will not depend AB on the size of the triangle. Hence we are correct in thinking of the ratio as belonging to the angle, not to the triangle. 

The ratio depends only on the angle. The ratio that we have just found is called the tangent of the angle A, usually written tan A.

Since triangles do not always have one line in a horizontal position and one vertical, another definition is as follows: 

In the right triangle ABC, we call 

AC the hypotenuse,

BC the side opposite angle A, AB the side adjacent to angle A. 

The tangent of angle A equals the ratio of the opposite side to the adjacent side, or 299. tan A the opposite side the adjacent side

302. Checking the Solution. The equations that we have written above are so simple that few mistakes occur in solving them. Nevertheless we need to check the solution because we may have written the wrong equation. To test the answer we may use a scale drawing as on page 294. For the problem on page 298, for example, draw a line to represent AB. Use a scale of 1 in. 100 ft. At A draw an angle of 40° and at B an angle of 90°. How long is BC in your drawing? In fact, it is advisable to make the drawing before writing the equation.

NUMERICAL TRIGONOMETRY 310. Interpolation. Further Use of the Tables. A de gree of an angle is divided into 60 minutes. Suppose we wish to find from the table tan 31° 30', or tan 3110. We see in the table that tan 31° = .601 tan 32° = .625

Since 31 is halfway between 31° and 32°, it would be natural to suppose that its tangent is halfway between .601 and .625.

In the same way, to find tan 31° 45', or tan 31°, we say that its tangent is three fourths of the way between .601 and .625. 

How do we find tan 31° 15'? 

How do we find tan 31° 20′?

Whenever an angle is stated in degrees and minutes, such a method must be used. We must therefore decide on some easy way to find a number which is a fractional part of the way between two other numbers. 

To find a number halfway between .601 and .625 we first subtract the smaller of these numbers from the greater. 

Although the difference (called the tabular difference) is .024 we need to remember only the figures 24. Half of this difference is 12 (actually it is .012), and this is to be added to the smaller number. Hence tan 31° 30′ = .601 + .012 = .613

EXAMPLE 1. Find tan 19° 12'. 

In the table tan 19° tan 20° .344 .364 

12' is of 60'. Hence we are to find the number which is of the way from .344 to .364. 

Call the tabular difference 20; of 20 = 4. 

Hence tan 19° 12' = .344 + .004 = .348.

EXAMPLE 2. Find sin 60° 24′. 

In the table 866 sin 60° sin 61° = .875 

24' is of 60'. The tabular difference is 9. of 9 is approximately 4 (using the nearest whole number). Hence sin 60° 24′ = .866 + .004 = .870.

NUMERICAL TRIGONOMETRY 

312. Approximate Numbers. If we measure the length of a rectangle and find that the length is closer to 17 in. than to either 16 in. or 18 in., we say we have measured the length to the nearest inch. If we measure with a ruler marked in tenths of an inch and find that the length is nearer to 17.3 in. than to either 17.2 or 17.4 in., we say that 17.3 in. is the length to the nearest tenth of an inch. We may call 17.3 in. the length of the rectangle, but we should recognize the fact that it is only an approximate number. Certain other numbers, such as π and the value in decimals of √2, √3, etc., are also stated only approximately. 

If the length of a line is 17.45 in., we call it 17.4 when using only tenths of an inch; but if the length is 17.55, we call it 17.6 because it is convenient, when possible, to have the last digit an even number. 

In doing this we assume that if many computations are made, we shall have increased the number about as often as we have decreased it. 

313. Accuracy of Multiplication of Approximate Numbers. 

EXAMPLE 1. What is the area of a rectangle 6.4 in. by 

2.7 in.?

If the length of a rectangle is 6.4 in. and the width is 2.7 in., the area of the rectangle according to the usual rules is (6.4 × 2.7) sq. in., or 

17.28 sq. in. But if 6.4 and 2.7 are approximate numbers, then the true length is some number between 6.35 and 

6.45, and the true width is some number between 

2.66 and 2.74. Hence the true area (as shown by the multiplications at the right) is between 

16.8910 and 17.6730. Since the area lies between these numbers it is incorrect to say that the area is 17.28.

We can also see this from the multiplication shown at the right. The doubtful figures are printed in heavy type. 

These figures might be changed if the lines were measured more carefully. Hence it would be better to say that the area is about 17, admitting that this, too, is an approximate number. 6.35 6.45 2.66 2.74 3810 2580 3810 4515 1270 1290 16.8910 17.6730 6.4 2.7 448 128 17.28

EXAMPLE 2. 14.87 X 263.4 

14.87 X 263.4 = 3916.758, but we call this product 3917.

CONSISTENT MEASUREMENTS AND TABLES 311 

= 3916758. 

EXAMPLE 3. 1487 × 2634 

Obviously we cannot call this product 3917, as in ex. 2. We call the product 3917000, inserting zeros for the figures which we have dropped.

In ex. 3, the figures 3, 9, 1, and 7 are called the significant figures of the number 3917000.

In all work we should use the following rule: 

When multiplying two approximate numbers do not keep more significant figures in the product than there are in that factor which has the smaller number of significant figures. 

If a number ends in zero, we cannot tell merely by looking at it how many significant figures it has. The number 180, for example, has three significant figures when it represents the sum of the angles of a triangle. It also has three significant figures if it represents the length of a field which is known to be between 179.5 ft. and 180.5 ft. 

But if the length of the field is between 175 ft. and 185 ft., then the number 180 has only two significant figures. Unless we have made the measurements ourselves we always need additional information stating how accurate the measurements are. 

314. Consistent Measurements and Tables. The above rule shows that we should not measure one line of a triangle less carefully than another line nor measure the angles less carefully than the lines. If the lines show 3 significant figures, the angles should be measured to the nearest tenth of a degree; and if the lines show 4 significant figures, the angles should be measured to the nearest hundredth of a degree (to the nearest minute usually in practice). 

Also, after making our measurements, we should be careful what kind of trigonometric table we use. If the lines and angles contain 3 significant figures, the table should contain 3 decimals. A table giving 4 decimals would merely give us more work in multiplying but not a more accurate result, as we may keep only 3 figures in any product. If the lines and angles are measured to 4 significant figures, we should use a table stating the ratios to 4 decimals, as on pages 314-319.

316. Historical Note. Hipparchus, a Greek astronomer who lived in the second century B.C., is credited with the invention of trigonometry. Although all his works are lost, historians believe he was the first to compute tables resembling our tables of sines. The Hindus improved on this work; but the greatest advances were made by a German, Regiomontanus (1436-1476). He traveled extensively in Europe in order to learn what the Greeks and Arabs had written on mathematics, and then wrote a complete textbook on trigonometry.

319. Reduction of Radicals. In many problems it is useful to change a radicand so that: (a) It is not divisible by a square, when finding square roots, nor divisible by a cube, when finding cube roots. (b) It is not a fraction.

CHAPTER XVIII SUPPLEMENTARY TOPICS FACTORING BY GROUPING TERMS 

326. To factor 3 ax + 3 ay + 5 bx + 5 by or any quantity containing more than three terms, we first look at the various terms to see in what ways they are alike or different. 

In the above quantity we notice that: 

The first two terms are both divisible by 3 a. 

The last two terms are both divisible by 5 b. Hence 3 ax + 3 ay + 5 bx +5 by = 3 a(x + y) + 5 b(x + y) This expression states that (x + y) is multiplied first by 3 a and then by 5 b. In other words, (x + y) is multiplied by (3 a +5b); or 

3 a(x + y) +5 b(x + y) = (3 a + 5 b) (x + y) 

EXAMPLE. 4 ax + 8 ay + 3 bx +6 by 327. 

4 a(x+2y) + 3 b(x + 2 y) (4 a + 3b) (x + 2 y)
